# Reed mat: the floor material of the first building tier

You are making one tiling material for a survival game in which players build bases out of square pieces (foundations, walls, doorways, ceilings) by the thousand. The cheapest tier is bundled reed thatch lashed to a frame of poles: light, uneven, quick to put up, and it should look it, and still be a pleasure to look at. This material is the floor of that tier: the top of every thatch foundation is a woven reed mat laid over a frame of logs. Players stand on it, drop their things on it and look down at it all the time, and from a distance a village of foundations shows a lot of it.

Work only in the current folder. Take as long as the job needs.

## What it shows

A coarse, hand-plaited floor mat of split reed or flattened rush, laid flat and seen straight from above.

- Strips about 3.75 cm wide, woven in a **two-over, two-under twill**, so the weave runs in shallow diagonals. Each strip is flat, with fine lengthwise fibres and the odd node or crease.
- Handmade: strips vary a little in width, hue and straightness; a few are split or frayed at an edge; the weave is not perfectly square everywhere. Some patches are worn paler and smoother where people walk; grit and dust sit in the hollows where strips cross. Not dilapidated: no holes, no rot, no mould.
- Colour: dry pale reed, khaki to light tan, a touch greyer and paler than golden straw so it reads as a floor next to the straw walls, with strips from cream to light brown. No green, nothing saturated. The mean colour of the base colour should be close to sRGB (178, 160, 118).
- No border, no binding, no mat edges: it is an endless weave. The frame of the piece covers its edges.

## The layout

- One tile covers **1.5 m by 1.5 m** of floor at **2048 by 2048 pixels** (about 0.73 mm a pixel).
- The strips run along the picture's rows and columns: **40 strips across the tile in each direction**, so the twill repeats exactly and tiles in both directions. Variation in the strips themselves must still wrap.

## What to deliver, all 2048 by 2048 and pixel-aligned (the same strip in the same place in every layer)

1. `mat_base.png`: base colour, sRGB, 8-bit RGB. Flat and unlit: no baked light, no cast shadow, no highlights, no occlusion darkening beyond the strips' real colour and the real grit.
2. `mat_height.png`: 16-bit greyscale height, white is high: each strip arching over the one it crosses and dipping under the next, the hollows between, the fibres. Say in your report how many millimetres the full range stands for (about 6 to 10 mm is right).
3. `mat_normal.png`: tangent-space normal map, **OpenGL convention** (green points to the top of the picture), computed from the delivered height with wrap-around at the physical scale above, so it agrees with the height.
4. `mat_orm.png`: one packed picture, linear, 8-bit RGB: **red** ambient occlusion (the hollows where strips cross), **green** roughness (dry reed about 0.7 to 0.9, the worn patches a little smoother), **blue** metalness (0 everywhere).

Keep the generated source pictures at full size in `source/`, and the scripts that build every layer (`build_material.py`) and check them (`check_material.py`) in this folder, so the whole thing can be rebuilt.

## What excellent looks like

The example to beat is the wood plank wall in the repository's `planning/artist-test/wood/`, which from this folder is `../../../../../../../planning/artist-test/wood/` (start with `checks/layers.jpg`, then `checks/tilings.jpg`, `checks/relighting.jpg` and `REPORT.md`; the pictures attached show it). It is a complete, aligned, tileable set with its own build and check scripts. Beat it: a floor of this should look woven by hand, hold up at full size, and not look like wallpaper when tiled three by three. A perfect mechanical weave is the failure to avoid: it must be regular enough to read as a weave and irregular enough to read as made by someone.

- The 2048 must hold real detail at full size: fibres in each strip, crisp crossings, not a blurred enlargement of a smaller picture. The image tool returns about 1254 pixels a side whatever is asked; reach 2048 by composing generated strips or patches into a larger master, by adding fine detail by script, or both.
- It tiles in both directions with no visible seam: check a half-offset with wrap-around and a 3 by 3 tiling, and fix what you see.
- No blemish or worn patch so distinctive that the eye finds its repeat in a 3 by 3 tiling.
- The layers agree: overlay or set them side by side and look. Relight the normal and occlusion from two or three directions and check that the crossings read the right way round.

## Checks to leave in `checks/`

A contact sheet of the four layers; the base colour tiled 3 by 3; the half-offset of each layer; a relit preview from at least two light directions; a 1:1 crop of the 2048 base colour and normal; and a JSON of your measurements (wrap seam against interior differences, normal against height agreement, base colour mean).

## Report

Finish by writing `REPORT.md`: for each file, how it was made (generated, edited, or computed by script), which checks you ran and what you saw, the metres per tile and the millimetres of the height range, how long the work took, and anything you are not satisfied with.

Do not use or imitate pictures from any existing game, and do not search for or download reference images: work from the description above.

The script registered the eight plank bands, quilted an overlapping horizontal period, corrected the vertical border gap, reduced fine relief contrast and edge darkening, and replaced the original shaded nail heads with smaller, flat iron reflectance. RGB, 8 bits/channel, embedded sRGB profile. | Viewed at full size, half-offset with wrapping, and in a 2×2 repeat. No perspective or overall lighting gradient. | `wood_height.png` | **Computed by script.** Shallow grain relief comes from locally normalized source luminance. Gaps are low, faces intermediate, nails high. Both repeat directions inspected. No geometry shift relative to the other layers. |

Angular difference: mean **0.177°**, maximum **0.382°**, consistent with RGB quantization. Viewed nail crops and opposite-light diagnostic previews; raised heads and gap bevels respond in the expected directions. |
| `wood_orm.png` | **Computed by script.** R: cavity AO from gap distance and shallow grain. G: approximately 0.73–0.94 roughness on wood, approximately 0.61 on iron. B: 255 on nail heads and 0 everywhere else. RGB 8-bit, linear data. | Viewed beside the other layers and in offset/repeat views. **Zero mismatches** between the blue channel and the nail mask. AO darkens cavities rather than reproducing whole-plank colour differences. |
| `wood_mask.png` | **Computed by script.** Shared categorical segmentation: plank faces red, six nail silhouettes green, gaps black. RGB 8-bit, linear data. | Exactly **three unique RGB values**: `(255,0,0)`, `(0,255,0)`, `(0,0,0)`. Six connected nail regions. No blended pixels at all. Inspected an albedo/mask outline overlay and enlarged crops of all six nails across all five maps. |
| `wood_base_2048.png` | **Edited/computed from the final albedo.** Periodic cubic reconstruction plus new coherent horizontal fibre detail, approximately 0.4–1.2 high-resolution pixels wide and 8–40 pixels long. Integer residual correction preserves every parent pixel's average. RGB 8-bit, embedded sRGB profile. | Compared native 2K crops with enlarged 1K crops, including gap edges. Extra fibre detail is present rather than only interpolated pixels. **Every 2×2 RGB block averages exactly to its 1K parent: maximum channel error 0.** Also inspected wrapped offsets and repeat views. |
| `wood_normal_2048.png` | **Computed from coarse surface slopes plus new fibre displacement.** The same fine fibre field used in colour supplies approximately 15-micrometre-scale displacement gradients. Each 2×2 block's mean direction is constrained to the original surface normal. OpenGL, RGB 8-bit. | After area averaging decoded vectors and renormalizing: mean difference **0.190°**, 99th percentile **0.404°**, maximum **0.535°** versus 1K. Raw RGB area-average error is **0.208/255 mean**, **3.25/255 maximum**. Inspected fine grain and the wrapped join at high resolution. |

The source image is retained as `source/generated_wood.png`. The built-in generator returned a 1254 × 1254 image despite the prompt's requested 2048 size. The final 2K additions are therefore explicitly procedural fibres, not a claim of a native 2K scan. The exact generation prompt is preserved in `generation_prompt.txt`. No fallback API/CLI generation was used.

The visual iteration found and fixed two related vertical-border issues. Initially the source contributed nearly a full gap at both ends, making the wrap gap average **9.56 pixels** wide. Narrowing it exposed a few bright source-border pixels that confused the boundary detector and bent nearby grain. Correcting the detector removed those distortions. The final wrap gap averages **4.01 pixels**, comparable to the other narrow plank gaps. I inspected the final half-offset albedo, 2×2 albedo repeat, high-resolution normal offset, and the layer/contact sheets. I see no obvious join requiring another seam repair.

Numerical seam checks compare the difference across the wrap with ordinary differences between adjacent interior columns. Every map's mean horizontal wrap difference is below that map's 95th-percentile interior-column difference. For example, the 1K albedo is **6.16/255** at the wrap versus **6.51/255** at the interior 95th percentile. Opposite edge pixels need not be identical: they represent adjacent samples of a continuous repeating surface. All filtering and derivative operations that affect delivered borders use wrapped boundaries.

The checks also verified PNG dimensions, channel counts, bit depth, the sRGB profiles on both albedos, absence of colour profiles on data maps, mask palette, metalness alignment, normal lengths, and both downsample comparisons. The exact albedo match uses a **box/area filter on encoded RGB values**; other resampling filters or linear-light resampling will give different numerical values. Normal reduction should average decoded vectors and renormalize.

Inspection artifacts are in `checks/`: `layers.jpg`, `alignment.jpg`, `nail_alignment.png`, `offsets.jpg`, `tilings.jpg`, `upscale_detail.png`, `relighting.jpg`, individual map offsets/repeats, and `metrics.json`. The diagnostic relighting images are simple normal/diffuse checks, not a complete metallic renderer. Their lighting is not baked into the delivered albedo. `derivation.npz` preserves the added fine displacement field and nail positions.

To reproduce the outputs with Python, Pillow, NumPy, and SciPy installed:

```sh
python3 build_material.py
python3 check_material.py
```

For engine import, enable sRGB only for the two base-colour files. Treat height, normals, ORM and mask as linear data, and enable repeat wrapping on both axes. The full grayscale height range represents **12 mm**; nominal faces sit near 0.56 and gaps near 0.095, giving approximately 5.6 mm of face-to-gap relief. The normals use this physical scale with the 2-metre tile coverage. Use nearest sampling if the categorical mask must retain pure colours when sampled.

Remaining limitations: this is an authored material, not measured photogrammetry. Grain height is inferred from local colour variation; AO and roughness are plausible procedural estimates rather than measured or ray-traced data. Fine pigment and relief separation in the generated wood is approximate. The few knots and nail placements become recognizable across large repeated walls. The 2K normals have the small quantization/downsampling differences reported above. I would ship this as a general game wall material; I have not evaluated it inside a particular game engine or under its texture compression settings.

# Image Generation Skill

Generates or edits images for the current project (for example website assets, game assets, UI mockups, product mockups, wireframes, logo design, photorealistic images, or infographics).

## Top-level modes and rules

This skill has exactly two top-level modes:

- **Default built-in tool mode (preferred):** built-in `image_gen` tool for image generation, editing, and transparent-image requests. Does not require `OPENAI_API_KEY`.
- **Fallback CLI mode:** `scripts/image_gen.py` CLI. Use when the user explicitly asks for or confirms the CLI/API/model path. Requires `OPENAI_API_KEY`.

Within CLI fallback, the CLI exposes three subcommands:

- `generate`
- `edit`
- `generate-batch`

Rules:
- Use the built-in `image_gen` tool by default for normal image generation and editing requests.
- Do not switch to CLI fallback for ordinary quality, size, or file-path control.
- For transparent images, ask built-in `image_gen` for a transparent background and preserve the generated alpha.
- Never silently switch from built-in `image_gen` or CLI `gpt-image-2` to CLI `gpt-image-1.5`; ask the user first unless they explicitly requested `gpt-image-1.5`.
- The word `batch` by itself does not mean CLI fallback. If the user asks for many assets or says to batch-generate assets without explicitly asking for CLI/API/model controls, stay on the built-in path and issue one built-in call per requested asset or variant.
- If the built-in tool fails or is unavailable, tell the user the CLI fallback exists and that it requires `OPENAI_API_KEY`. Proceed only if the user explicitly asks for that fallback.
- If the user explicitly asks for CLI mode, use the bundled `scripts/image_gen.py` workflow. Do not create one-off SDK runners.
- Never modify `scripts/image_gen.py`. If something is missing, ask the user before doing anything else.

Built-in save-path policy:
- In built-in tool mode, Codex saves generated images under `$CODEX_HOME/*` by default.
- Do not describe or rely on OS temp as the default built-in destination.
- Do not describe or rely on a destination-path argument (if any) on the built-in `image_gen` tool. If a specific location is needed, generate first and then move or copy the selected output from `$CODEX_HOME/generated_images/...`.
- Save-path precedence in built-in mode:
  1. If the user names a destination, move or copy the selected output there.
  2. If the image is meant for the current project, move or copy the final selected image into the workspace before finishing.
  3. If the image is only for preview or brainstorming, render it inline; the underlying file can remain at the default `$CODEX_HOME/*` path.
- Never leave a project-referenced asset only at the default `$CODEX_HOME/*` path.
- Do not overwrite an existing asset unless the user explicitly asked for replacement; otherwise create a sibling versioned filename such as `hero-v2.png` or `item-icon-edited.png`.

Shared prompt guidance for both modes lives in `references/prompting.md` and `references/sample-prompts.md`.

Fallback-only docs/resources for CLI mode:
- `references/cli.md`
- `references/image-api.md`
- `references/codex-network.md`
- `scripts/image_gen.py`

## When to use
- Generate a new image (concept art, product shot, cover, website hero)
- Generate a new image using one or more reference images for style, composition, or mood
- Edit an existing image (inpainting, lighting or weather transformations, background replacement, object removal, compositing, transparent background)
- Produce many assets or variants for one task

## When not to use
- Extending or matching an existing SVG/vector icon set, logo system, or illustration library inside the repo
- Creating simple shapes, diagrams, wireframes, or icons that are better produced directly in SVG, HTML/CSS, or canvas
- Making a small project-local asset edit when the source file already exists in an editable native format
- Any task where the user clearly wants deterministic code-native output instead of a generated bitmap

## Decision tree

Think about two separate questions:

1. **Intent:** is this a new image or an edit of an existing image?
2. **Execution strategy:** is this one asset or many assets/variants?

Intent:
- If the user wants to modify an existing image while preserving parts of it, treat the request as **edit**.
- If the user provides images only as references for style, composition, mood, or subject guidance, treat the request as **generate**.
- If the user provides no images, treat the request as **generate**.

Built-in edit semantics:
- Built-in edit mode is for images already visible in the conversation context, such as attached images or images generated earlier in the thread.
- If the user wants to edit a local image file with the built-in tool, first load it with built-in `view_image` tool so the image is visible in the conversation context, then proceed with the built-in edit flow.
- Do not promise arbitrary filesystem-path editing through the built-in tool.
- If a local file still needs direct file-path control, masks, or other explicit CLI-only parameters, use the explicit CLI fallback only when the user asks for it.
- For edits, preserve invariants aggressively and save non-destructively by default.

Execution strategy:
- In the built-in default path, produce many assets or variants by issuing one `image_gen` call per requested asset or variant.
- In the CLI fallback path, use the CLI `generate-batch` subcommand only when the user explicitly chose CLI mode and needs many prompts/assets.
- For many distinct assets, do not use `n` as a substitute for separate prompts. `n` is for variants of one prompt; distinct assets need distinct built-in calls or distinct CLI `generate-batch` jobs.

Assume the user wants a new image unless they clearly ask to change an existing one.

## Workflow
1. Decide the top-level mode: built-in by default, including transparent-output requests; fallback CLI only if explicitly requested or confirmed.
2. Decide the intent: `generate` or `edit`.
3. Decide whether the output is preview-only or meant to be consumed by the current project.
4. Decide the execution strategy: single asset vs repeated built-in calls vs CLI `generate-batch`.
5. Collect inputs up front: prompt(s), exact text (verbatim), constraints/avoid list, and any input images.
6. For every input image, label its role explicitly:
   - reference image
   - edit target
   - supporting insert/style/compositing input
7. If the edit target is only on the local filesystem and you are staying on the built-in path, inspect it with `view_image` first so the image is available in conversation context.
8. If the user asked for a photo, illustration, sprite, product image, banner, or other explicitly raster-style asset, use `image_gen` rather than substituting SVG/HTML/CSS placeholders. If the request is for an icon, logo, or UI graphic that should match existing repo-native SVG/vector/code assets, prefer editing those directly instead.
9. Augment the prompt based on specificity:
   - If the user's prompt is already specific and detailed, normalize it into a clear spec without adding creative requirements.
   - If the user's prompt is generic, add tasteful augmentation only when it materially improves output quality.
10. Use the built-in `image_gen` tool by default.
11. For transparent-output requests, ask built-in `image_gen` for a transparent background and preserve the generated alpha channel.
12. Inspect outputs and validate: subject, style, composition, text accuracy, and invariants/avoid items.
13. Iterate with a single targeted change, then re-check.
14. For preview-only work, render the image inline; the underlying file may remain at the default `$CODEX_HOME/generated_images/...` path.
15. For project-bound work, move or copy the selected artifact into the workspace and update any consuming code or references. Never leave a project-referenced asset only at the default `$CODEX_HOME/generated_images/...` path.
16. For batches or multi-asset requests, persist every requested deliverable final in the workspace unless the user explicitly asked to keep outputs preview-only. Discarded variants do not need to be kept unless requested.
17. If the user explicitly chooses or confirms the CLI fallback, then use the fallback-only docs for model, quality, size, `input_fidelity`, masks, output format, output paths, and network setup.
18. Always report the final saved path(s) for any workspace-bound asset(s), plus the final prompt or prompt set and whether the built-in tool or fallback CLI mode was used.

## Transparent image requests

Ask built-in `image_gen` for a genuinely transparent background and preserve its alpha.

## Prompt augmentation

Reformat user prompts into a structured, production-oriented spec. Make the user's goal clearer and more actionable, but do not blindly add detail.

Treat this as prompt-shaping guidance, not a closed schema. Use only the lines that help, and add a short extra labeled line when it materially improves clarity.

### Specificity policy

Use the user's prompt specificity to decide how much augmentation is appropriate:

- If the prompt is already specific and detailed, preserve that specificity and only normalize/structure it.
- If the prompt is generic, you may add tasteful augmentation when it will materially improve the result.

Allowed augmentations:
- composition or framing hints
- polish level or intended-use hints
- practical layout guidance
- reasonable scene concreteness that supports the stated request

Not allowed augmentations:
- extra characters or objects that are not implied by the request
- brand names, slogans, palettes, or narrative beats that are not implied
- arbitrary side-specific placement unless the surrounding layout supports it

## Use-case taxonomy (exact slugs)

Classify each request into one of these buckets and keep the slug consistent across prompts and references.

Generate:
- photorealistic-natural — candid/editorial lifestyle scenes with real texture and natural lighting.
- product-mockup — product/packaging shots, catalog imagery, merch concepts.
- ui-mockup — app/web interface mockups and wireframes; specify the desired fidelity.
- infographic-diagram — diagrams/infographics with structured layout and text.
- scientific-educational — classroom explainers, scientific diagrams, and learning visuals with required labels and accuracy constraints.
- ads-marketing — campaign concepts and ad creatives with audience, brand position, scene, and exact tagline/copy.
- productivity-visual — slide, chart, workflow, and data-heavy business visuals.
- logo-brand — logo/mark exploration, vector-friendly.
- illustration-story — comics, children’s book art, narrative scenes.
- stylized-concept — style-driven concept art, 3D/stylized renders.
- historical-scene — period-accurate/world-knowledge scenes.

Edit:
- text-localization — translate/replace in-image text, preserve layout.
- identity-preserve — try-on, person-in-scene; lock face/body/pose.
- precise-object-edit — remove/replace a specific element (including interior swaps).
- lighting-weather — time-of-day/season/atmosphere changes only.
- background-extraction — transparent background / clean cutout. Ask built-in `image_gen` for actual transparency.
- style-transfer — apply reference style while changing subject/scene.
- compositing — multi-image insert/merge with matched lighting/perspective.
- sketch-to-render — drawing/line art to photoreal render.

## Shared prompt schema

Use the following labeled spec as shared prompt scaffolding for both top-level modes:

```text
Use case: <taxonomy slug>
Asset type: <where the asset will be used>
Primary request: <user's main prompt>
Input images: <Image 1: role; Image 2: role> (optional)
Scene/backdrop: <environment>
Subject: <main subject>
Style/medium: <photo/illustration/3D/etc>
Composition/framing: <wide/close/top-down; placement>
Lighting/mood: <lighting + mood>
Color palette: <palette notes>
Materials/textures: <surface details>
Text (verbatim): "<exact text>"
Constraints: <must keep/must avoid>
Avoid: <negative constraints>
```

Notes:
- `Asset type` and `Input images` are prompt scaffolding, not dedicated CLI flags.
- `Scene/backdrop` refers to the visual setting. It is not the same as the fallback CLI `background` parameter, which controls output transparency behavior.
- Fallback-only execution notes such as `Quality:`, `Input fidelity:`, masks, output format, and output paths belong in the CLI path only. Do not treat them as built-in `image_gen` tool arguments.

Augmentation rules:
- Keep it short.
- Add only the details needed to improve the prompt materially.
- For edits, explicitly list invariants (`change only X; keep Y unchanged`).
- If any critical detail is missing and blocks success, ask a question; otherwise proceed.

## Examples

### Generation example (hero image)
```text
Use case: product-mockup
Asset type: landing page hero
Primary request: a minimal hero image of a ceramic coffee mug
Style/medium: clean product photography
Composition/framing: wide composition with usable negative space for page copy if needed
Lighting/mood: soft studio lighting
Constraints: no logos, no text, no watermark
```

### Edit example (invariants)
```text
Use case: precise-object-edit
Asset type: product photo background replacement
Primary request: replace only the background with a warm sunset gradient
Constraints: change only the background; keep the product and its edges unchanged; no text; no watermark
```

## Prompting best practices
- Structure prompt as scene/backdrop -> subject -> details -> constraints.
- Include intended use (ad, UI mock, infographic) to set the mode and polish level.
- Use camera/composition language for photorealism.
- Only use SVG/vector stand-ins when the user explicitly asked for vector output or a non-image placeholder.
- Quote exact text and specify typography + placement.
- For tricky words, spell them letter-by-letter and require verbatim rendering.
- For multi-image inputs, reference images by index and describe how they should be used.
- For edits, repeat invariants every iteration to reduce drift.
- Iterate with single-change follow-ups.
- If the prompt is generic, add only the extra detail that will materially help.
- If the prompt is already detailed, normalize it instead of expanding it.
- For CLI fallback only, see `references/cli.md` and `references/image-api.md` for model, `quality`, `input_fidelity`, masks, output format, and output-path guidance.
- For transparent images, ask built-in `image_gen` for actual transparency and preserve its alpha.

More principles shared by both modes: `references/prompting.md`.
Copy/paste specs shared by both modes: `references/sample-prompts.md`.

## Guidance by asset type
Asset-type templates (website assets, game assets, wireframes, logo) are consolidated in `references/sample-prompts.md`.

## gpt-image-2 guidance for CLI fallback

The fallback CLI defaults to `gpt-image-2`.

- Use `gpt-image-2` for new CLI/API workflows unless the user confirms a different model.
- CLI `gpt-image-2` does not support `background=transparent`; ask before using `gpt-image-1.5` unless the user explicitly requested that model.
- `gpt-image-2` always uses high fidelity for image inputs; do not set `input_fidelity` with this model.
- `gpt-image-2` supports `quality` values `low`, `medium`, `high`, and `auto`.
- Use `quality low` for fast drafts, thumbnails, and quick iterations. Use `medium`, `high`, or `auto` for final assets, dense text, diagrams, identity-sensitive edits, or high-resolution outputs.
- Square images are typically fastest to generate. Use `1024x1024` for fast square drafts.
- If the user asks for 4K-style output, use `3840x2160` for landscape or `2160x3840` for portrait.
- `gpt-image-2` size may be `auto` or `WIDTHxHEIGHT` if all constraints hold: max edge `<= 3840px`, both edges multiples of `16px`, long-to-short ratio `<= 3:1`, total pixels between `655,360` and `8,294,400`.

Popular `gpt-image-2` sizes:
- `1024x1024` square
- `1536x1024` landscape
- `1024x1536` portrait
- `2048x2048` 2K square
- `2048x1152` 2K landscape
- `3840x2160` 4K landscape
- `2160x3840` 4K portrait
- `auto`

## Fallback CLI mode only

### Temp and output conventions
These conventions apply only to the CLI fallback. They do not describe built-in `image_gen` output behavior.
- Use `tmp/imagegen/` for intermediate files (for example JSONL batches); delete them when done.
- Write final artifacts under `output/imagegen/`.
- Use `--out` or `--out-dir` to control output paths; keep filenames stable and descriptive.

### Dependencies
Prefer `uv` for dependency management in this repo.

Required Python package:
```bash
uv pip install openai
```

Optional for image inspection and downscaling:
```bash
uv pip install pillow
```

Portability note:
- If you are using the installed skill outside this repo, install dependencies into that environment with its package manager.
- In uv-managed environments, `uv pip install ...` remains the preferred path.

### Environment
- `OPENAI_API_KEY` must be set for live API calls.
- Do not ask the user for `OPENAI_API_KEY` when using the built-in `image_gen` tool.
- Never ask the user to paste the full key in chat. Ask them to set it locally and confirm when ready.

If the key is missing, give the user these steps:
1. Create an API key in the OpenAI platform UI: https://platform.openai.com/api-keys
2. Set `OPENAI_API_KEY` as an environment variable in their system.
3. Offer to guide them through setting the environment variable for their OS/shell if needed.

If installation is not possible in this environment, tell the user which dependency is missing and how to install it into their active environment.

### Script-mode notes
- CLI commands + examples: `references/cli.md`
- API parameter quick reference: `references/image-api.md`
- Network approvals / sandbox settings for CLI mode: `references/codex-network.md`

+Use case: photorealistic-natural.
+Asset type: original surface-detail source atlas for a survival game's dry split-reed floor material. This is NOT a finished woven mat; it is a sampling sheet of long flat reed ribbons.
+Primary request: a square, extremely detailed, orthographic straight-down scan of sixteen long, flattened split-reed / rush strips lying parallel horizontally, each going all the way from left to right edge. Fill the square. All fibres follow the horizontal long axis. Each ribbon has a slightly different natural cream, pale khaki, gray-beige or light tan pigment. Dry pale reed, desaturated, no green or orange. Subtle fibrous striations, occasional tiny transverse node creases and thin fraying at a few ribbon edges, fine flaking pith, slight abrasions, naturally uneven longitudinal pigment. Substantial fine real organic detail everywhere, not smooth or rendered plastic. Restrained variation, no distinctive dark marks, no holes, no rot.
+Composition: exactly overhead, flat, edge-to-edge crop of parallel horizontal split-reed ribbons; no perspective. No weave, no crossing ribbons, no borders, no binding, no props, no text.
+Lighting: diffuse cross-polarized scan, absolutely flat albedo-like surface, no directional lighting, no highlights, no cast shadows, no ambient occlusion between ribbons. Texture pigmentation only. Approximate mean sRGB 178,160,118. High resolution fine surface detail. Original invented material; do not reference any existing game.

"""Reproducible, periodic PBR derivation. Run from this material's directory."""
from pathlib import Path
import json
import numpy as np
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw, PngImagePlugin, ImageCms
from scipy.ndimage import gaussian_filter, map_coordinates, distance_transform_edt

ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parent
OUT = ROOT
CHECK = ROOT / 'checks'
CHECK.mkdir(exist_ok=True)
RNG = np.random.default_rng(48117)
N = 1024
HEIGHT_METRES = 0.012
SRGB = ImageCms.ImageCmsProfile(ImageCms.createProfile('sRGB')).tobytes()

def blur(a, sy, sx=None):
    return gaussian_filter(a, (sy, sy if sx is None else sx), mode='wrap')

def save(name, a, srgb=False):
    info = PngImagePlugin.PngInfo()
    info.add_text('Material', 'Weathered horizontal oak; tile coverage 2 x 2 metres')
    info.add_text('Color space', 'sRGB' if srgb else 'linear data; do not apply sRGB decoding')
    if 'normal' in name:
        info.add_text('Normal convention', 'OpenGL tangent space: +X right, +Y image-up, +Z out')
    if 'height' in name:
        info.add_text('Height scale', '0..1 = 0..0.012 metres; white is high')
    if 'orm' in name:
        info.add_text('Channels', 'R ambient occlusion; G roughness; B metalness')
    if a.dtype == np.uint16:
        im = Image.fromarray(a)
    else:
        im = Image.fromarray(np.uint8(np.clip(np.rint(a), 0, 255)))
    kw = {'icc_profile': SRGB} if srgb else {}
    im.save(OUT / name, pnginfo=info, **kw)

def resize(a, size, method=Image.Resampling.BICUBIC):
    if a.ndim == 2:
        return np.array(Image.fromarray(a.astype('float32')).resize((size, size), method))
    return np.stack([resize(a[..., i], size, method) for i in range(a.shape[-1])], -1)

def blockmean(a):
    return a.reshape(N, 2, N, 2, *a.shape[2:]).mean((1, 3))

def repeat(a):
    return a.repeat(2, 0).repeat(2, 1)

def normal(h, metres=HEIGHT_METRES):
    px = 2.0 / h.shape[0]
    dx = (np.roll(h, -1, 1) - np.roll(h, 1, 1)) * metres / (2 * px)
    dy = (np.roll(h, -1, 0) - np.roll(h, 1, 0)) * metres / (2 * px)
    # The array's y increases down; the texture tangent's +Y increases up.
    v = np.stack([-dx, dy, np.ones_like(h)], -1)
    return v / np.linalg.norm(v, axis=-1, keepdims=True)

def periodic_resize(a, target):
    # Sample pixel centres using wrapped cubic interpolation, with no clamped borders.
    yy, xx = np.meshgrid((np.arange(target) + .5) * a.shape[0] / target - .5,
                         (np.arange(target) + .5) * a.shape[1] / target - .5, indexing='ij')
    if a.ndim == 2:
        return map_coordinates(a, [yy, xx], order=3, mode='grid-wrap')
    return np.stack([map_coordinates(a[..., c], [yy, xx], order=3, mode='grid-wrap')
                     for c in range(a.shape[2])], -1)

def quilt_periodic_x(src, overlap=160):
    """Cut through an overlapping period; edges follow adjacent source samples."""
    h, w, _ = src.shape
    period = w - overlap
    left, right = src[:, :overlap], src[:, period:]
    error = blur(((left - right) ** 2).mean(2), 2, 2)
    # Keep the optimal cut away from overlap boundaries for a short feather.
    error[:, :20] += 1e6
    error[:, -20:] += 1e6
    cumulative = error[0].copy()
    predecessor = np.zeros((h, overlap), dtype='int16')
    for y in range(1, h):
        choices = np.stack([np.r_[1e20, cumulative[:-1]], cumulative,
                            np.r_[cumulative[1:], 1e20]])
        step = choices.argmin(0) - 1
        predecessor[y] = np.arange(overlap) + step
        cumulative = error[y] + choices.min(0)
    path = np.zeros(h, dtype=int)
    path[-1] = np.argmin(cumulative)
    for y in range(h-2, -1, -1):
        path[y] = predecessor[y+1, path[y+1]]
    weight = np.clip((np.arange(overlap)[None] - path[:, None]) / 14 + .5, 0, 1)
    weight = weight * weight * (3 - 2 * weight)
    result = src[:, :period].copy()
    result[:, :overlap] = right * (1 - weight[..., None]) + left * weight[..., None]
    return result

src = np.asarray(Image.open(ROOT / 'source/generated_wood.png').convert('RGB'), dtype=float)
S = src.shape[0]
lum = src.mean(2)
gap_centres = [0, 172, 345, 514, 680, 840, 990, 1123, S]
# Flatten and rescale the generated nail heads. A same-plank neighbouring patch
# removes the original lit rim and shadow before matte iron is applied below.
source_nails = [(812, 84), (231, 254), (952, 596), (410, 766), (962, 920), (219, 1057)]
sy, sx = np.mgrid[:S, :S]
for x, y in source_nails:
    radius = np.hypot(sx-x, sy-y)
    w = np.clip((radius - 15) / 9, 0, 1)
    w = w * w * (3 - 2*w)
    patch = np.roll(src, 46, axis=1)
    region = radius < 24
    src[region] = src[region] * w[region, None] + patch[region] * (1-w[region, None])

# Register each generated plank to a 128-pixel band. Detect the dark gap centre
# independently in each column, preserving the small rough edge variations.
bounds = np.zeros((9, S))
# The generator drew almost a full gap at BOTH vertical borders. Crop each to
# a half-gap before registration, otherwise the tiled join is visibly too wide.
first_face = np.argmax(lum[:20] > 34, axis=0)
last_face = S-20+np.argmax(lum[-20:] < 34, axis=0)-1
bounds[0] = gaussian_filter(np.maximum(0,first_face-2.8).astype(float),1,mode='wrap')
bounds[-1] = gaussian_filter(np.minimum(S,last_face+2.2).astype(float),1,mode='wrap')
for i, centre in enumerate(gap_centres[1:-1], 1):
    ys = np.arange(centre-7, centre+8)
    weights = np.maximum(38 - lum[ys], 0)
    bounds[i] = (weights * ys[:, None]).sum(0) / np.maximum(weights.sum(0), .001)
    bounds[i] = gaussian_filter(bounds[i], 1.0, mode='wrap')
yy, xx = np.mgrid[:N, :S]
row = np.minimum(yy // 128, 7)
fraction = (yy % 128) / 128
source_y = bounds[row, xx] * (1-fraction) + bounds[row+1, xx] * fraction
warped = np.stack([map_coordinates(src[..., c], [source_y, xx], order=1, mode='wrap') for c in range(3)], -1)
periodic = quilt_periodic_x(warped)
period = periodic.shape[1]
coords_y, coords_x = np.meshgrid(np.arange(N), (np.arange(N)+.5)*period/N-.5, indexing='ij')
base = np.stack([map_coordinates(periodic[..., c], [coords_y, coords_x], order=3, mode='grid-wrap') for c in range(3)], -1)
base = np.clip(base, 0, 255)
grey = (base * np.array([.2126, .7152, .0722])).sum(2)

# One categorical segmentation drives all five delivered maps.
gap = (grey < 34) & (np.minimum(np.arange(N) % 128, 128-np.arange(N) % 128)[:, None] < 8)
# Close the vertical period inside the physical void, not across plank faces.
gap[0:2] = True
gap[-2:] = True
for j in range(1,8):
    gap[j*128-1:j*128+2] = True
distance = distance_transform_edt(np.tile(~gap, (3,3)))[N:2*N, N:2*N]
wood = ~gap

# Reduce fine relief lighting in the source albedo and remove directional edge
# darkening. Preserve grain pigment and the subtle grey-brown board differences.
smooth = np.stack([blur(base[..., c], .75, .4) for c in range(3)], -1)
base = .72*base + .28*smooth
local = np.stack([blur(base[..., c], 10, 26) for c in range(3)], -1)
near_edge = (distance < 5) & wood
for j in range(8):
    top = j*128
    for d in range(1,5):
        for row0, row1 in [(top+d, top+9), ((top-d)%N, (top-9)%N)]:
            sel = near_edge[row0]
            mix = .45*(1-d/5)
            base[row0, sel] = base[row0, sel]*(1-mix) + base[row1, sel]*mix
base[gap] = np.array([30., 24., 19.])

Y, X = np.mgrid[:N, :N]
metal = np.zeros((N,N), bool)

# Reed mat: the floor material of the first building tier

You are making one tiling material for a survival game in which players build bases out of square pieces (foundations, walls, doorways, ceilings) by the thousand. The cheapest tier is bundled reed thatch lashed to a frame of poles: light, uneven, quick to put up, and it should look it, and still be a pleasure to look at. This material is the floor of that tier: the top of every thatch foundation is a woven reed mat laid over a frame of logs. Players stand on it, drop their things on it and look down at it all the time, and from a distance a village of foundations shows a lot of it.

Work only in the current folder. Take as long as the job needs.

## What it shows

A coarse, hand-plaited floor mat of split reed or flattened rush, laid flat and seen straight from above.

- Strips about 3.75 cm wide, woven in a **two-over, two-under twill**, so the weave runs in shallow diagonals. Each strip is flat, with fine lengthwise fibres and the odd node or crease.
- Handmade: strips vary a little in width, hue and straightness; a few are split or frayed at an edge; the weave is not perfectly square everywhere. Some patches are worn paler and smoother where people walk; grit and dust sit in the hollows where strips cross. Not dilapidated: no holes, no rot, no mould.
- Colour: dry pale reed, khaki to light tan, a touch greyer and paler than golden straw so it reads as a floor next to the straw walls, with strips from cream to light brown. No green, nothing saturated. The mean colour of the base colour should be close to sRGB (178, 160, 118).
- No border, no binding, no mat edges: it is an endless weave. The frame of the piece covers its edges.

## The layout

- One tile covers **1.5 m by 1.5 m** of floor at **2048 by 2048 pixels** (about 0.73 mm a pixel).
- The strips run along the picture's rows and columns: **40 strips across the tile in each direction**, so the twill repeats exactly and tiles in both directions. Variation in the strips themselves must still wrap.

## What to deliver, all 2048 by 2048 and pixel-aligned (the same strip in the same place in every layer)

1. `mat_base.png`: base colour, sRGB, 8-bit RGB. Flat and unlit: no baked light, no cast shadow, no highlights, no occlusion darkening beyond the strips' real colour and the real grit.
2. `mat_height.png`: 16-bit greyscale height, white is high: each strip arching over the one it crosses and dipping under the next, the hollows between, the fibres. Say in your report how many millimetres the full range stands for (about 6 to 10 mm is right).
3. `mat_normal.png`: tangent-space normal map, **OpenGL convention** (green points to the top of the picture), computed from the delivered height with wrap-around at the physical scale above, so it agrees with the height.
4. `mat_orm.png`: one packed picture, linear, 8-bit RGB: **red** ambient occlusion (the hollows where strips cross), **green** roughness (dry reed about 0.7 to 0.9, the worn patches a little smoother), **blue** metalness (0 everywhere).

Keep the generated source pictures at full size in `source/`, and the scripts that build every layer (`build_material.py`) and check them (`check_material.py`) in this folder, so the whole thing can be rebuilt.

## What excellent looks like

The example to beat is the wood plank wall in the repository's `planning/artist-test/wood/`, which from this folder is `../../../../../../../planning/artist-test/wood/` (start with `checks/layers.jpg`, then `checks/tilings.jpg`, `checks/relighting.jpg` and `REPORT.md`; the pictures attached show it). It is a complete, aligned, tileable set with its own build and check scripts. Beat it: a floor of this should look woven by hand, hold up at full size, and not look like wallpaper when tiled three by three. A perfect mechanical weave is the failure to avoid: it must be regular enough to read as a weave and irregular enough to read as made by someone.

- The 2048 must hold real detail at full size: fibres in each strip, crisp crossings, not a blurred enlargement of a smaller picture. The image tool returns about 1254 pixels a side whatever is asked; reach 2048 by composing generated strips or patches into a larger master, by adding fine detail by script, or both.
- It tiles in both directions with no visible seam: check a half-offset with wrap-around and a 3 by 3 tiling, and fix what you see.
- No blemish or worn patch so distinctive that the eye finds its repeat in a 3 by 3 tiling.
- The layers agree: overlay or set them side by side and look. Relight the normal and occlusion from two or three directions and check that the crossings read the right way round.

## Checks to leave in `checks/`

A contact sheet of the four layers; the base colour tiled 3 by 3; the half-offset of each layer; a relit preview from at least two light directions; a 1:1 crop of the 2048 base colour and normal; and a JSON of your measurements (wrap seam against interior differences, normal against height agreement, base colour mean).

## Report

Finish by writing `REPORT.md`: for each file, how it was made (generated, edited, or computed by script), which checks you ran and what you saw, the metres per tile and the millimetres of the height range, how long the work took, and anything you are not satisfied with.

Do not use or imitate pictures from any existing game, and do not search for or download reference images: work from the description above.

index 0000000000000000000000000000000000000000..1db10b1dfc7df882642b9da09ec727c2e3d6fcbd
--- /dev/null
+++ b/assets/source/core/structures/thatch/tex/mat/build_material.py
@@ -0,0 +1,260 @@
+#!/usr/bin/env python3
+"""Build an original, periodic 40 x 40 split-reed 2/2 twill at native 2048.
+
+Requires Python 3, NumPy, SciPy and Pillow. No network access. All paths are
+relative to this script, and all outputs stay in this material directory.
+"""
+from pathlib import Path
+import hashlib
+import json
+import time
+
+import numpy as np
+from PIL import Image, ImageCms, PngImagePlugin
+from scipy.ndimage import gaussian_filter, map_coordinates
+
+ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parent
+N = 2048
+STRIPS = 40
+TILE_M = 1.5
+HEIGHT_MM = 8.0
+SEED = 617803
+TARGET_RGB = np.array([178., 160., 118.], dtype=np.float32)
+SRGB = ImageCms.ImageCmsProfile(ImageCms.createProfile('sRGB')).tobytes()
+
+
+def smoothstep(a, b, x):
+    t = np.clip((x-a)/(b-a), 0., 1.)
+    return t*t*(3.-2.*t)
+
+
+def blur(a, sigma):
+    return gaussian_filter(a, sigma, mode='wrap')
+
+
+def noise(rng, shape, sigma):
+    a = blur(rng.standard_normal(shape, dtype=np.float32), sigma)
+    return (a - a.mean()) / max(float(a.std()), 1.e-6)
+
+
+def save_png(name, a, srgb=False, extra=None):
+    info = PngImagePlugin.PngInfo()
+    info.add_text('Material', 'Hand-plaited split reed; 2-over / 2-under twill; 40 strips per axis')
+    info.add_text('Physical size', '1.5 x 1.5 metres; repeat X and Y')
+    info.add_text('Color space', 'sRGB' if srgb else 'linear data; do not decode as sRGB')
+    for k, v in (extra or {}).items():
+        info.add_text(k, v)
+    kwargs = {'icc_profile': SRGB} if srgb else {}
+    Image.fromarray(a).save(ROOT / name, pnginfo=info, **kwargs)
+
+
+def rgb8(a):
+    return np.clip(np.rint(a), 0, 255).astype(np.uint8)
+
+
+def normal_from_height(h16):
+    """Central differences of the DELIVERED uint16 height; +Y is image-up."""
+    h = h16.astype(np.float32) * (HEIGHT_MM / 65535.)
+    px_mm = TILE_M * 1000. / N
+    dx = (np.roll(h, -1, 1) - np.roll(h, 1, 1)) / (2. * px_mm)
+    dy_down = (np.roll(h, -1, 0) - np.roll(h, 1, 0)) / (2. * px_mm)
+    normal = np.stack([-dx, dy_down, np.ones_like(h)], axis=-1)
+    normal /= np.linalg.norm(normal, axis=-1, keepdims=True)
+    return rgb8((normal * .5 + .5) * 255.)
+
+
+def make_atlas(rng, src):
+    """80 independent ribbon surfaces, 64 samples wide, 2048 along the fibre.
+
+    Three native-density source patches are blended around each ribbon's ring.
+    Source ribbon edges are excluded. Slow and cross-ribbon illumination is
+    removed before the residual is used as low-amplitude pigment detail.
+    Native pixel-scale procedural fibres provide independent relief and pigment.
+    """
+    bounds = np.array([0, 86, 173, 256, 338, 420, 502, 584,
+                       667, 749, 829, 909, 986, 1058, 1131, 1199, 1254])
+    bounds = bounds * src.shape[0] / 1254.
+    cy, ax = np.mgrid[:64, :N].astype(np.float32)
+    palettes = rng.normal(0, 7.2, 80)
+    palettes -= palettes.mean()
+    hue = rng.normal(0, 2., (80, 3)).astype(np.float32)
+    colour = np.empty((80, 64, N, 3), dtype=np.float32)
+    relief = np.empty((80, 64, N), dtype=np.float32)
+    fibre_saved = np.empty_like(relief)
+    for k in range(80):
+        detail = np.zeros((64, N), dtype=np.float32)
+        tint = np.zeros_like(detail)
+        for p in range(3):
+            row = int(rng.integers(0, 16))
+            margin = 13.
+            ys = bounds[row] + margin + (cy+.5)/64.*(bounds[row+1]-bounds[row]-2*margin)
+            # Each patch is active only within +/- 683 pixels of its centre.
+            # Choose a 900 px periodic source track with a 200 px crossfade.
+            phase = rng.uniform(0, 900)
+            xs = (ax + phase) % 900
+            left = map_coordinates(src.mean(2), [ys, xs+160], order=1, mode='nearest')
+            right = map_coordinates(src.mean(2), [ys, xs+1060], order=1, mode='nearest')
+            w = smoothstep(0., 190., xs)
+            track = left*w + right*(1.-w)
+            # The right sample only contributes during the first 190 pixels,
+            # where xs+1060 <=1250. Adjacent ring endpoints remain continuous.
+            track -= blur(track, (7., 70.))
+            track -= track.mean(1, keepdims=True) * .45
+            # Raised-cosine partition of unity, periodic across output ends.
+            dist = ((ax - (p/3.*N) + N/2.) % N) - N/2.
+            weight = (1.+np.cos(np.pi*np.clip(dist/(N/3.), -1., 1.)))/2.
+            detail += track * weight
+        detail *= .47
+        coarse = noise(rng, (64, N), (1.8, 39.))
+        fine = noise(rng, (64, N), (.48, 12.))
+        fine2 = noise(rng, (64, N), (.85, 3.4))
+        drift = 1.4*np.sin(2*np.pi*ax/N*3+rng.uniform(0, 6.28))
+        drift += .55*np.sin(2*np.pi*ax/N*11+rng.uniform(0, 6.28))
+        fine = map_coordinates(fine, [(cy+drift)%64, ax], order=1, mode='grid-wrap')
+        broad = noise(rng, (64, N), (9., 110.))
+        fiber = .040*coarse + .026*fine + .013*fine2
+        # Gentle short transverse nodes and small edge splits, unique per ribbon.
+        node = np.zeros_like(detail)
+        splits = np.zeros_like(detail)
+        for _ in range(int(rng.integers(1, 4))):
+            cx = rng.uniform(0, N)
+            distance = ((ax-cx+N/2.)%N)-N/2.
+            bend = (cy-32.) * rng.uniform(-.12, .12)
+            node += np.exp(-((distance+bend)/rng.uniform(.8, 1.9))**2/2.) * rng.uniform(.025, .065)
+        if rng.random() < .60:
+            cx = rng.uniform(0, N)
+            distance = ((ax-cx+N/2.)%N)-N/2.
+            taper = np.exp(-.5*(distance/rng.uniform(40, 105))**2)
+            edge_y = rng.choice([7., 56.]) + 1.1*np.sin(distance/65.)
+            splits = np.exp(-.5*((cy-edge_y)/.5)**2)*taper
+        # Stochastic lines are pigment/relief, not directional edge shading.
+        gray = detail + 2.3*coarse + 1.45*fine + .65*fine2 + 1.8*broad
+        gray += 12.*node - 5.*splits
+        colour[k] = TARGET_RGB + palettes[k]*np.array([1., .98, .85]) + hue[k]
+        colour[k] += gray[..., None] * np.array([1., .98, .85])
+        relief[k] = fiber + node - .16*splits + detail*.003
+        fibre_saved[k] = fiber
+    return colour.reshape(80*64, N, 3), relief.reshape(80*64, N), fibre_saved.reshape(80*64, N)
+
+
+def horizon_ao(h):
+    """Periodic heightfield horizon integration, twelve azimuths, eight radii."""
+    px_mm = TILE_M * 1000./N
+    ao = np.zeros_like(h)
+    for angle in np.arange(12)*2*np.pi/12:
+        slope = np.zeros_like(h)
+        seen = set()
+        for radius in [1, 2, 3, 5, 9, 16, 30, 58]:
+            dx, dy = int(round(np.cos(angle)*radius)), int(round(np.sin(angle)*radius))
+            if (dx, dy) in seen or (dx == 0 and dy == 0):
+                continue
+            seen.add((dx, dy))
+            sample = np.roll(h, (dy, dx), axis=(0, 1))
+            slope = np.maximum(slope, (sample-h)/(np.hypot(dx,dy)*px_mm))
+        ao += 1./(1.+slope*slope)
+    return np.clip(ao/12., 0., 1.)
+
+
+def main():
+    started = time.time()
+    rng = np.random.default_rng(SEED)
+    print('Building eighty native-density ribbon surfaces...', flush=True)
+    src = np.asarray(Image.open(ROOT/'source/generated_reed_atlas.png').convert('RGB'), dtype=np.float32)
+    atlas, grain_atlas, fine_atlas = make_atlas(rng, src)
+    # Retain full-resolution assembled source sheets for inspection and provenance.
+    save_png('source/composed_ribbon_surfaces.png', rgb8(atlas), srgb=True)
+    yy, xx = np.mgrid[:N, :N].astype(np.float32)
+    pitch = N/STRIPS
+    u = xx/pitch + .37
+    v = yy/pitch + .21
+    # Periodic deformations retain the 40-strip winding number on both axes.
+    # A broad warp and smaller irregularities avoid a machine-square basket grid.
+    u += .105*noise(rng, (N,N), 105.) + .035*noise(rng, (N,N), 23.)
+    v += .105*noise(rng, (N,N), 105.) + .035*noise(rng, (N,N), 23.)
+    column_offsets = rng.uniform(-.13, .13, 40).astype(np.float32)
+    row_offsets = rng.uniform(-.13, .13, 40).astype(np.float32)
+    u += map_coordinates(column_offsets, [(xx/pitch).ravel()], order=3, mode='grid-wrap').reshape(N,N)
+    v += map_coordinates(row_offsets, [(yy/pitch).ravel()], order=3, mode='grid-wrap').reshape(N,N)
+    ui, vi = np.floor(u).astype(np.int32), np.floor(v).astype(np.int32)
+    uf, vf = u-ui, v-vi
+    hcoords = [(vi%40)*64 + np.clip(vf*64, .51, 63.49), (u/40.*N)%N]
+    vcoords = [((ui%40)+40)*64 + np.clip(uf*64, .51, 63.49), (v/40.*N)%N]
+    print('Plaiting 40 x 40, with periodic width and alignment variation...', flush=True)
+    col_h = np.stack([map_coordinates(atlas[...,c], hcoords, order=1, mode='grid-wrap') for c in range(3)], -1)
+    col_v = np.stack([map_coordinates(atlas[...,c], vcoords, order=1, mode='grid-wrap') for c in range(3)], -1)
+    relief_h = map_coordinates(grain_atlas, hcoords, order=1, mode='grid-wrap')
+    relief_v = map_coordinates(grain_atlas, vcoords, order=1, mode='grid-wrap')
+    native_fibre_h = map_coordinates(fine_atlas, hcoords, order=1, mode='grid-wrap')
+    native_fibre_v = map_coordinates(fine_atlas, vcoords, order=1, mode='grid-wrap')
+    del atlas, grain_atlas, fine_atlas
+    wear = smoothstep(-.5, 2.0, .73*noise(rng, (N,N), 108.) + .27*noise(rng, (N,N), 39.))
+    # Ragged edges stay below a pixel on average; the weave remains intact.
+    edge_h = .25*noise(rng, (N,N), (4., 1.7)) + .23*noise(rng,(N,N), (8.,12.))
+    edge_v = .25*noise(rng, (N,N), (1.7,4.)) + .23*noise(rng,(N,N), (12.,8.))
+    dh = (np.minimum(vf,1.-vf)*pitch) - .64 + edge_h
+    dv = (np.minimum(uf,1.-uf)*pitch) - .64 + edge_v
+    cover_h = smoothstep(-.6,.6,dh)
+    cover_v = smoothstep(-.6,.6,dv)
+    phase_h = np.cos(np.pi/2.*(u-vi-1.))
+    phase_v = -np.cos(np.pi/2.*(ui-v))
+    arch_h = np.tanh(phase_h*1.7)/np.tanh(1.7)
+    arch_v = np.tanh(phase_v*1.7)/np.tanh(1.7)
+    thickness_h = rng.uniform(-.10,.10,40)[vi%40]
+    thickness_v = rng.uniform(-.10,.10,40)[ui%40]
+    # Flat ribbons, a very slight cross-section crown, soft bent exits at crossings.
+    base_z = 3.45 + .10*noise(rng,(N,N),52.)
+    h_raw = base_z + 1.28*arch_h + thickness_h + .13*np.sin(np.pi*vf)
+    v_raw = base_z + 1.28*arch_v + thickness_v + .13*np.sin(np.pi*uf)
+    h_raw -= .33*np.exp(-np.maximum(dh,0)/1.25)
+    v_raw -= .33*np.exp(-np.maximum(dv,0)/1.25)
+    h_raw += relief_h*(1.-.37*wear)
+    v_raw += relief_v*(1.-.37*wear)
+    ground_z = 1.32 + .035*noise(rng,(N,N),(.8,.8))
+    zh = ground_z + cover_h*(h_raw-ground_z)
+    zv = ground_z + cover_v*(v_raw-ground_z)
+    top_h = smoothstep(-.065,.065,zh-zv).astype(np.float32)
+    coverage = np.maximum(cover_h,cover_v)
+    height = blur(np.maximum(zh,zv).astype(np.float32), .39)
+    print('Computing heightfield cavity occlusion and native fine detail...', flush=True)
+    ao = horizon_ao(height)
+    cavity = smoothstep(.04,.39,1.-ao)
+    grit_rng = rng.random((N,N), dtype=np.float32)
+    grit = blur((grit_rng > .987).astype(np.float32),.52)*2.5*cavity
+    dust = cavity*(.28+.25*smoothstep(-1.,1.,noise(rng,(N,N),5.)))
+    base = col_h*top_h[...,None] + col_v*(1.-top_h[...,None])
+    del col_h, col_v
+    # Only real deposited dust/grit affects albedo at crossings. AO is separate.
+    base = base*coverage[...,None] + np.array([158.,145.,113.])*(1.-coverage[...,None])
+    base += wear[...,None]*np.array([3.8,4.1,4.8])
+    base = base*(1.-dust[...,None]*.10) + np.array([168.,154.,122.])*dust[...,None]*.10
+    base -= grit[...,None]*np.array([12.,11.,8.])
+    height += grit*.07
+    base += TARGET_RGB - base.mean((0,1))
+    roughness = .827 + .017*noise(rng,(N,N),9.) - .115*wear + .041*cavity
+    roughness += .010*noise(rng,(N,N),.65)
+    roughness = np.clip(roughness,.66,.92)
+    h16 = np.rint(np.clip(height/HEIGHT_MM,0,1)*65535).astype(np.uint16)
+    # Recompute AO after the tiny grit relief, using the quantized delivered height.
+    ao = horizon_ao(h16.astype(np.float32)*HEIGHT_MM/65535.)
+    save_png('mat_base.png',rgb8(base),srgb=True)
+    save_png('mat_height.png',h16,extra={'Height scale':'0..65535 = 0..8 millimetres; white is high'})
+    save_png('mat_normal.png',normal_from_height(h16),extra={'Normal convention':'OpenGL: +X right, +Y image-up, +Z out; central differences with wrap'})
+    save_png('mat_orm.png',rgb8(np.stack([ao,roughness,np.zeros_like(ao)],-1)*255),
+             extra={'Channels':'R ambient occlusion; G roughness; B metalness (zero)'})
+    # These are diagnostic fields, not additional engine textures.
+    np.savez_compressed(ROOT/'source/derivation.npz',u=u.astype(np.float32),v=v.astype(np.float32),
+        horizontal_top=top_h,wear=wear.astype(np.float32),coverage=coverage.astype(np.float32),
+        native_fibre_mm=(native_fibre_h*top_h+native_fibre_v*(1.-top_h)).astype(np.float32))
+    meta = {'seed':SEED,'resolution':[N,N],'strips_per_axis':STRIPS,'tile_metres':[TILE_M,TILE_M],
+        'pitch_mm':TILE_M*1000/STRIPS,'pixel_mm':TILE_M*1000/N,'height_range_mm':HEIGHT_MM,
+        'used_height_mm':[float(h16.min()*HEIGHT_MM/65535),float(h16.max()*HEIGHT_MM/65535)],
+        'source_size':list(Image.open(ROOT/'source/generated_reed_atlas.png').size),
+        'source_sha256':hashlib.sha256((ROOT/'source/generated_reed_atlas.png').read_bytes()).hexdigest(),
+        'normal_method':'wrapped central differences of delivered uint16; encode normalized (-dH/dx,+dH/drow,1)',
+        'build_seconds':round(time.time()-started,2)}
+    (ROOT/'source/build_metadata.json').write_text(json.dumps(meta,indent=2)+'\n')
+    print(json.dumps(meta,indent=2),flush=True)
+
+
+if __name__ == '__main__':
+    main()

+Use case: photorealistic-natural.
+Asset type: original surface-detail source atlas for a survival game's dry split-reed floor material. This is NOT a finished woven mat; it is a sampling sheet of long flat reed ribbons.
+Primary request: a square, extremely detailed, orthographic straight-down scan of sixteen long, flattened split-reed / rush strips lying parallel horizontally, each going all the way from left to right edge. Fill the square. All fibres follow the horizontal long axis. Each ribbon has a slightly different natural cream, pale khaki, gray-beige or light tan pigment. Dry pale reed, desaturated, no green or orange. Subtle fibrous striations, occasional tiny transverse node creases and thin fraying at a few ribbon edges, fine flaking pith, slight abrasions, naturally uneven longitudinal pigment. Substantial fine real organic detail everywhere, not smooth or rendered plastic. Restrained variation, no distinctive dark marks, no holes, no rot.
+Composition: exactly overhead, flat, edge-to-edge crop of parallel horizontal split-reed ribbons; no perspective. No weave, no crossing ribbons, no borders, no binding, no props, no text.
+Lighting: diffuse cross-polarized scan, absolutely flat albedo-like surface, no directional lighting, no highlights, no cast shadows, no ambient occlusion between ribbons. Texture pigmentation only. Approximate mean sRGB 178,160,118. High resolution fine surface detail. Original invented material; do not reference any existing game.

+    info.add_text('Color space', 'sRGB' if srgb else 'linear data; do not decode as sRGB')
+    for k, v in (extra or {}).items():
+        info.add_text(k, v)
+    kwargs = {'icc_profile': SRGB} if srgb else {}
+    Image.fromarray(a).save(ROOT / name, pnginfo=info, **kwargs)
+

+

+    dy_down = (np.roll(h, -1, 0) - np.roll(h, 1, 0)) / (2. * px_mm)
+    normal = np.stack([-dx, dy_down, np.ones_like(h)], axis=-1)
+    normal /= np.linalg.norm(normal, axis=-1, keepdims=True)
+    return rgb8((normal * .5 + .5) * 255.)
+
+

+    Source ribbon edges are excluded. Slow and cross-ribbon illumination is
+    removed before the residual is used as low-amplitude pigment detail.
+    Native pixel-scale procedural fibres provide independent relief and pigment.
+    """
+    bounds = np.array([0, 86, 173, 256, 338, 420, 502, 584,
+                       667, 749, 829, 909, 986, 1058, 1131, 1199, 1254])
+    bounds = bounds * src.shape[0] / 1254.

+    palettes = rng.normal(0, 7.2, 80)
+    palettes -= palettes.mean()

+    colour = np.empty((80, 64, N, 3), dtype=np.float32)
+    relief = np.empty((80, 64, N), dtype=np.float32)
+    fibre_saved = np.empty_like(relief)
+    for k in range(80):

+        tint = np.zeros_like(detail)
+        for p in range(3):

+            margin = 13.

+            # Choose a 900 px periodic source track with a 200 px crossfade.

+            # The right sample only contributes during the first 190 pixels,
+            # where xs+1060 <=1250. Adjacent ring endpoints remain continuous.
+            track -= blur(track, (7., 70.))
+            track -= track.mean(1, keepdims=True) * .45
+            # Raised-cosine partition of unity, periodic across output ends.
+            dist = ((ax - (p/3.*N) + N/2.) % N) - N/2.
+            weight = (1.+np.cos(np.pi*np.clip(dist/(N/3.), -1., 1.)))/2.
+            detail += track * weight
+        detail *= .47
+        coarse = noise(rng, (64, N), (1.8, 39.))
+        fine = noise(rng, (64, N), (.48, 12.))
+        fine2 = noise(rng, (64, N), (.85, 3.4))
+        drift = 1.4*np.sin(2*np.pi*ax/N*3+rng.uniform(0, 6.28))
+        drift += .55*np.sin(2*np.pi*ax/N*11+rng.uniform(0, 6.28))
+        fine = map_coordinates(fine, [(cy+drift)%64, ax], order=1, mode='grid-wrap')
+        broad = noise(rng, (64, N), (9., 110.))
+        fiber = .040*coarse + .026*fine + .013*fine2
+        # Gentle short transverse nodes and small edge splits, unique per ribbon.
+        node = np.zeros_like(detail)
+        splits = np.zeros_like(detail)
+        for _ in range(int(rng.integers(1, 4))):
+            cx = rng.uniform(0, N)
+            distance = ((ax-cx+N/2.)%N)-N/2.
+            bend = (cy-32.) * rng.uniform(-.12, .12)
+            node += np.exp(-((distance+bend)/rng.uniform(.8, 1.9))**2/2.) * rng.uniform(.025, .065)
+        if rng.random() < .60:
+            cx = rng.uniform(0, N)
+            distance = ((ax-cx+N/2.)%N)-N/2.
+            taper = np.exp(-.5*(distance/rng.uniform(40, 105))**2)
+            edge_y = rng.choice([7., 56.]) + 1.1*np.sin(distance/65.)
+            splits = np.exp(-.5*((cy-edge_y)/.5)**2)*taper
+        # Stochastic lines are pigment/relief, not directional edge shading.
+        gray = detail + 2.3*coarse + 1.45*fine + .65*fine2 + 1.8*broad
+        gray += 12.*node - 5.*splits
+        colour[k] = TARGET_RGB + palettes[k]*np.array([1., .98, .85]) + hue[k]
+        colour[k] += gray[..., None] * np.array([1., .98, .85])
+        relief[k] = fiber + node - .16*splits + detail*.003
+        fibre_saved[k] = fiber
+    return colour.reshape(80*64, N, 3), relief.reshape(80*64, N), fibre_saved.reshape(80*64, N)
+
+
+def horizon_ao(h):
+    """Periodic heightfield horizon integration, twelve azimuths, eight radii."""
+    px_mm = TILE_M * 1000./N
+    ao = np.zeros_like(h)
+    for angle in np.arange(12)*2*np.pi/12:
+        slope = np.zeros_like(h)
+        seen = set()
+        for radius in [1, 2, 3, 5, 9, 16, 30, 58]:
+            dx, dy = int(round(np.cos(angle)*radius)), int(round(np.sin(angle)*radius))
+            if (dx, dy) in seen or (dx == 0 and dy == 0):
+                continue
+            seen.add((dx, dy))
+            sample = np.roll(h, (dy, dx), axis=(0, 1))
+            slope = np.maximum(slope, (sample-h)/(np.hypot(dx,dy)*px_mm))
+        ao += 1./(1.+slope*slope)
+    return np.clip(ao/12., 0., 1.)
+
+
+def main():
+    started = time.time()
+    rng = np.random.default_rng(SEED)
+    print('Building eighty native-density ribbon surfaces...', flush=True)
+    src = np.asarray(Image.open(ROOT/'source/generated_reed_atlas.png').convert('RGB'), dtype=np.float32)
+    atlas, grain_atlas, fine_atlas = make_atlas(rng, src)
+    # Retain full-resolution assembled source sheets for inspection and provenance.
+    save_png('source/composed_ribbon_surfaces.png', rgb8(atlas), srgb=True)
+    yy, xx = np.mgrid[:N, :N].astype(np.float32)
+    pitch = N/STRIPS
+    u = xx/pitch + .37
+    v = yy/pitch + .21
+    # Periodic deformations retain the 40-strip winding number on both axes.
+    # A broad warp and smaller irregularities avoid a machine-square basket grid.
+    u += .105*noise(rng, (N,N), 105.) + .035*noise(rng, (N,N), 23.)
+    v += .105*noise(rng, (N,N), 105.) + .035*noise(rng, (N,N), 23.)
+    column_offsets = rng.uniform(-.13, .13, 40).astype(np.float32)
+    row_offsets = rng.uniform(-.13, .13, 40).astype(np.float32)
+    u += map_coordinates(column_offsets, [(xx/pitch).ravel()], order=3, mode='grid-wrap').reshape(N,N)
+    v += map_coordinates(row_offsets, [(yy/pitch).ravel()], order=3, mode='grid-wrap').reshape(N,N)
+    ui, vi = np.floor(u).astype(np.int32), np.floor(v).astype(np.int32)
+    uf, vf = u-ui, v-vi
+    hcoords = [(vi%40)*64 + np.clip(vf*64, .51, 63.49), (u/40.*N)%N]
+    vcoords = [((ui%40)+40)*64 + np.clip(uf*64, .51, 63.49), (v/40.*N)%N]
+    print('Plaiting 40 x 40, with periodic width and alignment variation...', flush=True)
+    col_h = np.stack([map_coordinates(atlas[...,c], hcoords, order=1, mode='grid-wrap') for c in range(3)], -1)
+    col_v = np.stack([map_coordinates(atlas[...,c], vcoords, order=1, mode='grid-wrap') for c in range(3)], -1)
+    relief_h = map_coordinates(grain_atlas, hcoords, order=1, mode='grid-wrap')
+    relief_v = map_coordinates(grain_atlas, vcoords, order=1, mode='grid-wrap')
+    native_fibre_h = map_coordinates(fine_atlas, hcoords, order=1, mode='grid-wrap')
+    native_fibre_v = map_coordinates(fine_atlas, vcoords, order=1, mode='grid-wrap')
+    del atlas, grain_atlas, fine_atlas
+    wear = smoothstep(-.5, 2.0, .73*noise(rng, (N,N), 108.) + .27*noise(rng, (N,N), 39.))
+    # Ragged edges stay below a pixel on average; the weave remains intact.
+    edge_h = .25*noise(rng, (N,N), (4., 1.7)) + .23*noise(rng,(N,N), (8.,12.))
+    edge_v = .25*noise(rng, (N,N), (1.7,4.)) + .23*noise(rng,(N,N), (12.,8.))
+    dh = (np.minimum(vf,1.-vf)*pitch) - .64 + edge_h
+    dv = (np.minimum(uf,1.-uf)*pitch) - .64 + edge_v
+    cover_h = smoothstep(-.6,.6,dh)
+    cover_v = smoothstep(-.6,.6,dv)
+    phase_h = np.cos(np.pi/2.*(u-vi-1.))
+    phase_v = -np.cos(np.pi/2.*(ui-v))
+    arch_h = np.tanh(phase_h*1.7)/np.tanh(1.7)
+    arch_v = np.tanh(phase_v*1.7)/np.tanh(1.7)
+    thickness_h = rng.uniform(-.10,.10,40)[vi%40]
+    thickness_v = rng.uniform(-.10,.10,40)[ui%40]
+    # Flat ribbons, a very slight cross-section crown, soft bent exits at crossings.
+    base_z = 3.45 + .10*noise(rng,(N,N),52.)
+    h_raw = base_z + 1.28*arch_h + thickness_h + .13*np.sin(np.pi*vf)
+    v_raw = base_z + 1.28*arch_v + thickness_v + .13*np.sin(np.pi*uf)
+    h_raw -= .33*np.exp(-np.maximum(dh,0)/1.25)
+    v_raw -= .33*np.exp(-np.maximum(dv,0)/1.25)
+    h_raw += relief_h*(1.-.37*wear)
+    v_raw += relief_v*(1.-.37*wear)
+    ground_z = 1.32 + .035*noise(rng,(N,N),(.8,.8))
+    zh = ground_z + cover_h*(h_raw-ground_z)
+    zv = ground_z + cover_v*(v_raw-ground_z)
+    top_h = smoothstep(-.065,.065,zh-zv).astype(np.float32)
+    coverage = np.maximum(cover_h,cover_v)
+    height = blur(np.maximum(zh,zv).astype(np.float32), .39)
+    print('Computing heightfield cavity occlusion and native fine detail...', flush=True)
+    ao = horizon_ao(height)
+    cavity = smoothstep(.04,.39,1.-ao)
+    grit_rng = rng.random((N,N), dtype=np.float32)
+    grit = blur((grit_rng > .987).astype(np.float32),.52)*2.5*cavity
+    dust = cavity*(.28+.25*smoothstep(-1.,1.,noise(rng,(N,N),5.)))
+    base = col_h*top_h[...,None] + col_v*(1.-top_h[...,None])
+    del col_h, col_v
+    # Only real deposited dust/grit affects albedo at crossings. AO is separate.
+    base = base*coverage[...,None] + np.array([158.,145.,113.])*(1.-coverage[...,None])
+    base += wear[...,None]*np.array([3.8,4.1,4.8])
+    base = base*(1.-dust[...,None]*.10) + np.array([168.,154.,122.])*dust[...,None]*.10
+    base -= grit[...,None]*np.array([12.,11.,8.])
+    height += grit*.07
+    base += TARGET_RGB - base.mean((0,1))
+    roughness = .827 + .017*noise(rng,(N,N),9.) - .115*wear + .041*cavity
+    roughness += .010*noise(rng,(N,N),.65)
+    roughness = np.clip(roughness,.66,.92)
+    h16 = np.rint(np.clip(height/HEIGHT_MM,0,1)*65535).astype(np.uint16)
+    # Recompute AO after the tiny grit relief, using the quantized delivered height.
+    ao = horizon_ao(h16.astype(np.float32)*HEIGHT_MM/65535.)
+    save_png('mat_base.png',rgb8(base),srgb=True)
+    save_png('mat_height.png',h16,extra={'Height scale':'0..65535 = 0..8 millimetres; white is high'})
+    save_png('mat_normal.png',normal_from_height(h16),extra={'Normal convention':'OpenGL: +X right, +Y image-up, +Z out; central differences with wrap'})
+    save_png('mat_orm.png',rgb8(np.stack([ao,roughness,np.zeros_like(ao)],-1)*255),
+             extra={'Channels':'R ambient occlusion; G roughness; B metalness (zero)'})
+    # These are diagnostic fields, not additional engine textures.
+    np.savez_compressed(ROOT/'source/derivation.npz',u=u.astype(np.float32),v=v.astype(np.float32),
+        horizontal_top=top_h,wear=wear.astype(np.float32),coverage=coverage.astype(np.float32),
+        native_fibre_mm=(native_fibre_h*top_h+native_fibre_v*(1.-top_h)).astype(np.float32))
+    meta = {'seed':SEED,'resolution':[N,N],'strips_per_axis':STRIPS,'tile_metres':[TILE_M,TILE_M],
+        'pitch_mm':TILE_M*1000/STRIPS,'pixel_mm':TILE_M*1000/N,'height_range_mm':HEIGHT_MM,
+        'used_height_mm':[float(h16.min()*HEIGHT_MM/65535),float(h16.max()*HEIGHT_MM/65535)],
+        'source_size':list(Image.open(ROOT/'source/generated_reed_atlas.png').size),
+        'source_sha256':hashlib.sha256((ROOT/'source/generated_reed_atlas.png').read_bytes()).hexdigest(),
+        'normal_method':'wrapped central differences of delivered uint16; encode normalized (-dH/dx,+dH/drow,1)',
+        'build_seconds':round(time.time()-started,2)}
+    (ROOT/'source/build_metadata.json').write_text(json.dumps(meta,indent=2)+'\n')
+    print(json.dumps(meta,indent=2),flush=True)
+
+
+if __name__ == '__main__':
+    main()

+Use case: photorealistic-natural.
+Asset type: original surface-detail source atlas for a survival game's dry split-reed floor material. This is NOT a finished woven mat; it is a sampling sheet of long flat reed ribbons.
+Primary request: a square, extremely detailed, orthographic straight-down scan of sixteen long, flattened split-reed / rush strips lying parallel horizontally, each going all the way from left to right edge. Fill the square. All fibres follow the horizontal long axis. Each ribbon has a slightly different natural cream, pale khaki, gray-beige or light tan pigment. Dry pale reed, desaturated, no green or orange. Subtle fibrous striations, occasional tiny transverse node creases and thin fraying at a few ribbon edges, fine flaking pith, slight abrasions, naturally uneven longitudinal pigment. Substantial fine real organic detail everywhere, not smooth or rendered plastic. Restrained variation, no distinctive dark marks, no holes, no rot.
+Composition: exactly overhead, flat, edge-to-edge crop of parallel horizontal split-reed ribbons; no perspective. No weave, no crossing ribbons, no borders, no binding, no props, no text.
+Lighting: diffuse cross-polarized scan, absolutely flat albedo-like surface, no directional lighting, no highlights, no cast shadows, no ambient occlusion between ribbons. Texture pigmentation only. Approximate mean sRGB 178,160,118. High resolution fine surface detail. Original invented material; do not reference any existing game.
